The results reveal substantial variability in responses to data visualisation and supervised learning tasks, particularly at medium and hard difficulty levels, highlighting the importance of evaluating consistency alongside response quality. We also find that an increase of 0.1 in the word verbosity ratio corresponds to an increase of about 0.013 in the token verbosity ratio.</p></details> |  |
| **[MemoryDojo: Benchmarking Poisoning Through LLM Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09721v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents benefit from using memory systems to improve performance on long-horizon, multi-session tasks. Through our evaluation, we find that all memory systems are susceptible to memory poisoning, with substantial variation in downstream effects. | **[Intent-Aware Caching for Efficient LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09715v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM serving systems use prefix caching to accelerate multi-turn conversational workloads. However, achieving a high cache hit rate remains challenging because cache reuse depends on whether a conversation continues and how soon the next turn arrives. Our analysis of real workloads shows that user intent is a useful signal for this behavior, as different intents exhibit different continuation probabilities and inter-turn delays. Based on this key insight, we propose Intent-Aware Caching (IAC), an online eviction policy that classifies requests by intent, learns lightweight reuse statistics from recent serving logs and leverages these statistics to guide an oracle-inspired eviction strategy. Extensive evaluation on real workloads (WildChat and ShareChat) shows that IAC reaches a 34.6% cache hit rate. This substantially outperforms both the previous learning-based approach (18.8%) and traditional methods such as least recently used (LRU, 12.2%), while reducing average serving latency by up to 46.1%.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 | Code: https://github.com/hasanar1f/intent-aware-caching</p></details> |
| **[Verifier Noise Is a Temperature: Testing Tilt Predictions in Finite-Budget LLM-Guided Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09699v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-guided search loops propose a modified candidate, score it, and keep it if it scores better. When both candidates are re-scored at every comparison, as in the minibatch acceptance gates of prompt optimizers, keep-if-better is a reversible Markov chain if the score difference is logistic and the mutation operator is reversible, and its stationary law is the mutation equilibrium tilted by exp(beta f). For any continuous symmetric noise difference the same tilt is the stationary law of a small-step diffusion limit, with beta = 4F'(0); for a minibatch gate beta grows with the square root of the minibatch size. Caching the incumbent's score instead inflates the recorded score. We test the tilt as a prediction for LLM-guided program search, committing each prediction from calibration data before the search runs. The level after 150 steps rises with the minibatch size in six of seven settings, but its predicted value fails: on bin packing, search attains 62-88% of the predicted gain when the proposer edits the parent and 109-119% when it writes new heuristics. The attained fraction falls with the parent-child quality correlation of unselected mutation; a registered test on two new settings confirmed the direction, but not the quantitative agreement after a correction. Further registered tests found that runs started from a good program kept losing quality over 300 steps, more so from starts that search had selected, by a margin that passed its registered rule narrowly. Runs from different starts had not reached a common level, so whether the tilt describes the stationary law of LLM editing remains open. A registered test in prompt optimisation on frozen task-model scores failed: search exceeded the tilt, and the bin-packing relation did not transfer.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 5 figures. Pre-registered predictions, code and data checksums are described in Appendix C</p></details> |

Our method proceeds in two stages: (1) querying the model with difficult factual questions across a diverse set of languages to generate "success" and "failure" activation buckets and then (2) applying a statistical contrastive analysis to the MoE router logits to identify experts important for knowledge. To validate the necessity of this small number of experts for answering a knowledge question, we deactivate them and re-ask the question. We find that despite only deactivating about 20 out of 6000 experts, the model no longer answers correctly in over 40% of cases. Generally, this method provides a realistic and scalable knowledge localization approach to address increasingly complex LLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026, https://openreview.net/forum?id=LAPZ4kgdnc</p></details> |
| **[Models Recall What They Violate: Constraint Adherence in Multi-Turn LLM Ideation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.28031v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When researchers refine ideas with large language models over several turns, do the models preserve the original brief's constraints? Iterative pressure to improve a proposal raises structural complexity in every model and lowers adherence to the brief's hard constraints. | **[ReplayAudit: Auditing Outsourced LLM Fine-Tuning via Sealed Records and Selective Replay](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09607v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Auditing outsourced LLM fine-tuning requires checking an untrusted provider's training while controlling verification cost. Loss changes and parameter-update norms guide stratified random sampling; replay determines whether the selected computation agrees with its records. We relate sampling probabilities to replay work and evaluate the system on real LoRA training. For two learning-rate deviation settings, executing these budgets on the same honest 1.5B trajectory reduces median complete-audit wall time by 49% and 67% relative to uniform sampling. When skipped updates evade the high-anomaly stratum, the corresponding audit instead costs 38% more. These results show how deviation capture and actual replay work jointly determine the cost of telemetry-guided auditing.</p></details> | 23 pages, 5 figures |
| **[On the Effectiveness and Limitations of Open-Weight LLMs for Low-Resource Offensive Language Detection in Danish](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09506v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study the effectiveness and robustness of open-weight large language models (LLMs) for Danish offensive language detection, an underexplored low-resource task. Semantically grounded prompts transfer more robustly, while format-constrained patterns consistently fail to generalize. ASCII transliteration of Danish-specific characters (æ, ø, å) generally degrades performance, indicating these characters encode meaningful signal. At the same time, the gap between a balanced test set (best F1 of 0.84) and the corpus's natural class skew (0.62 trained on skewed data, 0.70 trained on balanced data) -- relative drops of 26.2\% and 16.7\% -- shows that class imbalance remains a critical open challenge. | **[RouteAlign: Aligning Task Requirements with Model Profiles for LLM Service Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08162v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM service selection requires performance evidence that reflects the demands of the current task. Workload and phase further distinguish these trends, motivating supervision guided by trajectory conditions. | **[Reformulating KV Cache Eviction Problem for Long-Context LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07234v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) support long-context inference but suffer from substantial memory and runtime overhead due to Key-Value (KV) Cache growth. In this work, we reformulate KV Cache eviction from a conventional head-wise, weight-averaging approach into an output-aware, layer-wise matrix multiplication approximation problem. Building on this metric, we propose the first unified eviction strategy that assigns globally comparable importance scores to tokens, enabling model-wide selection instead of local, head-wise decisions. Experimental results across 19 datasets on long-context benchmarks LongBench and Needle-In-A-Haystack demonstrate that our approach maintains model performance with only 5% of the KV cache and consistently outperforms prior works across all configurations. Notably, our method achieves up to 2$\times$ accuracy loss reduction under extreme compression scenarios compared to existing state-of-the-art baselines with minimal overhead. | **[Nebula: Distributed Position-Independent Caching for LLM Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09474v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Modern LLM workflows, such as retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) and LLM agents, build long prompts from multiple independent text segments, making prefill a major source of computation. Reusing KV caches is therefore important for reducing serving costs and latency: distributed caching systems spread KV cache across a cluster to increase aggregate capacity, while position-independent caching (PIC) enables reuse of recurring segments under different prefixes. However, existing systems distribute KV caches but still rely on prefix caching (PC), while existing PIC methods enable position-independent reuse without cluster-wide resource management. | **[LLMs as Post-hoc Auditors of Physiological Plausibility in Symbolic Regression: A Clinician-Evaluated Case Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.11431v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genetic programming and its variants, such as grammatical evolution, are widely used in symbolic regression to derive mathematical expressions from multivariate data. Building on our previous work on estimating body fat percentage using grammar-based genetic programming, we investigate LLMs as post-processing tools to analyze and rank evolved expressions by interpretability and physiological plausibility. | **[Beyond Truncation? SocialAgent Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[LLM-Augmented Flow for Project Conformation and Task Complexity Evaluation Based on the TLM Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08931v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The software development process, from inception to a working product or solution, can be divided into two major phases: discovery (Discovery) and delivery (Delivery). Across eight tasks spanning reasoning, creative generation, and social bias mitigation, curiosity-guided multi-agent settings yield improvements over non-curious ones on seven out of eight tasks, with gains of up to 20.6% in story generation (semantic diversity) and a 14.9 pp reduction in stereotype rates. Furthermore, country-conditioned cultural grounding show benefits for story generation and social tasks, but can hurt precision-oriented reasoning. Finally, we show that inference-time multi-agent curiosity-based approach offers favorable quality-token trade-offs, compared with single-agent baselines. Together, these findings support curiosity-guided interaction as a practical mechanism for improving multi-agent collaboration, with downstream benefits for reasoning, creative, and societal tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Prepr...</summary><p>Preprint under review</p></details> |
| **[Wind Turbine Maintenance Log Labelling Framework: LLM-Driven Data Correction and Enrichment via Semantic Extraction of Reliability Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31281v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inconsistent codes and unstructured text limit the use of wind turbine maintenance records in reliability analysis. Failure-mode profiles linked labels to common mechanisms, possible symptoms and candidate causes. Schema constraints, deterministic rules and source links supported auditable processing. Proposed labels required model-reported High confidence and no human-review flag. Accepted system-code updates covered 2,178 of 2,984 targeted records; accepted failure-mode labels covered 11,661 records (71.5%). The enriched fields separated pitch interventions from broader system categories and distinguished electrical and hydraulic pitch maintenance patterns. Recorded task durations totalled 6.83 hours. A source-based assessment covered 800 records selected by balanced probability sampling. Changed system codes and maintenance types required source support; retained specific labels were approved unless contradicted. Equal-weight mean approval across four fields was 86.3%: 94.8% for system codes, 87.3% for maintenance types, 85.6% for actions and 77.5% for failure-mode outcomes. Failure-mode assessment covered titles and unresolved, unmatched or inapplicable statuses. Qualitative reviews identified unsupported actions and unclear category boundaries. The structured records provide traceable evidence for engineering review and future operational linkage supporting event reconstruction, exposure-based reliability analysis and failure modes and effects analysis.</p></details> | <details><summary>An ad...</summary><p>An adjustable template containing the Python script architecture, applied dynamic prompts, and data schemas is hosted in an open-source GitHub repository: https://github.com/mvmalyi/llm-driven-wind-turbine-maintenance-log-labelling</p></details> |
| **[Structuring Survey Histories for LLM Digital Twins: A Controlled Study of Background, Decision Procedure, and Evaluation Profiles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08730v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based digital twins aim to predict how individuals would respond in new settings from representations of their prior responses. In a paired evaluation with 100 respondents, a fully inferred BDE profile improves overall accuracy over the raw survey transcript by 1.27 percentage points (pp) on gpt-5.4-nano (p < 0.01) and 1.19 pp on gpt-5.4-mini (p < 0.001). Three analyses then examine the schema, extraction depth, and component composition. Against a length-matched free-form profile written from the same transcript by the same extractor, BDE improves pricing accuracy on both simulators (2.15 pp on nano, p < 0.01; 1.38 pp on mini, p < 0.05), while its overall advantage is significant only on nano (1.05 pp, p < 0.05). By contrast, adding Evaluation to a Background--Decision procedure profile improves overall accuracy by 1.44 pp on nano (p < 0.001) and 1.22 pp on mini (p < 0.001). | **[Memory-Aware Multi-Agent Architecture with LLM-based Orchestration for Conversational Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08718v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational recommender systems often treat user sessions in isolation, limiting their ability to capture how user intent evolves over time. We evaluate the system through cross-session memory experiments, multi-turn conversational simulations, and supervisor-driven interaction studies on the Amazon Reviews dataset. Results show that memory-enabled interactions recover up to 63\% of the gap toward explicit-query scenarios and prevent semantic degradation in extended dialogues. In addition, supervisory orchestration increases user acceptance by +28.9 percentage points, highlighting the importance of structured orchestration in guiding interaction and output quality. We also observe an exposure bottleneck between retrieved candidates and displayed recommendations. While LLM-reranking does not improve Hit@K, it consistently enhances semantic consistency and diversity. To capture these effects, we introduce the Semantic Consistency Score (SCS), a complementary evaluation measure. Overall, the results emphasize the importance of system-level design choices, suggesting that effective conversational recommendation benefits from explicit memory integration, orchestration, and evaluation beyond traditional ranking metrics.</p></details> |  |

On a real-world e-commerce fraud detector trained on selection-biased data, continued training regresses even when carefully tuned, whereas the overlay gains on average +2.26% recall at equal precision ($Δ$R@P) and +1.60% precision at equal recall ($Δ$P@R) over production cut-offs spanning 30-57% precision and 23-63% recall. Three measurements anchor the paper. First, the two tested models, run unbounded with the allowed action sets stated in the prompt, still violated intent-handler rules in 13 of 44 standard calls. Every violation broke an intent-handler combination rule that the deployed flat schema cannot encode (schema enforcement as deployed caught 0/13); a union schema encoding the rule removes the class by construction. Second, with the bounds in place, all 33 decoded live plans were feasible without repair, and a matched-condition probe put the in-distribution unbounded rate at one violation in 20 windows. Third, exploratory: on the design set the planner matched an author-specified selection reference that a threshold policy and three a priori codings each missed. | **[Dual-Stream LLM-Guided Reward Design for Reject-Inference-Aware Reinforcement Learning in E-Commerce Payment Fraud Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08706v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Production fraud-detection models learn from a censored slice of their own traffic: transactions a legacy system blocked never receive fraud labels, so models train on a biased, approved-only distribution. (2025) in which an LLM evolves the reward of a two-step REINFORCE fraud-blocking policy, to attack this reject-inference problem inside the reward. A single LLM designs a dual-stream reward: stream 1 applies the standard dollar-weighted precision objective to labeled transactions; stream 2 scores legacy-blocked, unlabeled transactions by contrasting the newest gradient-boosted risk signal against the legacy scores that drove the original block. Rather than prescribe which signals matter, we expose a superset of evidence and let the LLM decide for itself which to use, guided by evaluation feedback alone; because it writes the reward as code, that choice is directly auditable. On a held-out four-month out-of-time test, the dual-stream agent beats the prior labeled-only agent at every target recall, by +4.7, +2.4 and +3.0 points of dollar-weighted precision at 80%, 85% and 90% recall, and the production baseline by +6.8 points on average. | **[Cancellation Is Not Enough: What Retries Leave Behind in an Overloaded LLM Engine](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08684v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Clients and proxies in front of an LLM engine retry calls that time out. After a transient overload, these retries can keep the engine from recovering even when calls arrive below its capacity. Since the engine may keep computing for clients that have left, propagating cancellation looks like the natural remedy. In this paper, we test it on vLLM with overload experiments on three open models, and with scheduler traces of one that follow each client attempt to the execution it creates in the engine. We find that with retries on, cancellation alone did not restore service in any run, and only 12-38% of the calls that arrived after the overload were delivered. The traces suggest why: retries that vLLM ran typically started prefill less than half a second before they timed out. Executions that vLLM later aborted took 79-85% of the prompt tokens it computed, and vLLM's prompt-token counter missed a third to half of all computed prompt tokens. A retry budget restored service by shedding retries but left many of the overload's calls undelivered. Request coalescing delivered every call from the overload onward, some late, unless cancellation had already aborted the running execution. Overload controls should thus be judged by the executions that retries add and the calls that clients receive.</p></details> | <details><summary>13 pa...</summary><p>13 pages, 6 figures, 3 tables</p></details> |
| **[From Senses to Decisions: The Information Flow of Auditory and Visual Perception in Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.10147v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can listen and see, but how do audio and visual signals actually travel through the network to shape an answer? In this study, we examine audio-visual information flow inside Audio-Visual Large Language Models (AVLLMs), tracing how AVLLMs route, utilize, and integrate audio and visual information across two input configurations, audio-visual video and multiple interleaved audio-visual items. We find that for audio-visual video, AVLLMs follow the sequential information flow pathway established for VLMs and VideoLLMs, with audio and visual contribution flowing along this pathway in proportion to the task's reliance on each modality. In settings with multiple interleaved audio-visual items, this routing shifts to different parallel streams. Furthermore, we demonstrate that audio-visual and other token types can be discarded once their information is transferred to LLM, with minimal impact on the model's prediction or even slight improvement, generalizing across multiple tasks and datasets, enabling more efficient inference. These findings hold across multiple models and scales, Qwen2.5-Omni and Video-SALMONN2 Plus at 3B and 7B scales, leading to hypotheses on why these flow structures emerge. Together, these results deliver the first coherent picture of how AVLLMs orchestrate sound and sight inside the network and lay the groundwork for the next wave of interpretability, design, and efficiency advances in audio-visual and broader MLLMs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by NeurIPS 2026, 54 pages, 34 figures</p></details> |
| **[Prompt Lottery in LLM-as-a-Judge: Measuring and Mitigating Sensitivity to Syntactic Rephrasing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.38076v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-as-a-Judge has become a standard for evaluating large language models, yet most approaches implicitly assume that a single judge prompt functions as a stable evaluation instrument. We introduce \textit{Prompt Lottery}, a phenomenon in which paraphrastic rephrasings produce different LLMaJ judgments. Across a large-scale study spanning diverse evaluation tasks, judge families, and model backbones, we find that these variations can alter evaluation outcomes and, in some settings, reverse relative model rankings. A user study with 10 human annotators shows that LLMaJ judges exhibit 6-9$\times$ larger agreement degradation under prompt rephrasings than humans, falling below the generally accepted threshold in usability studies. | **[MORTIS: Compiler-Guided KV-Cache Management for LLM Agent Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08638v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing agent serving systems divide each prompt into a reusable prefix and an opaque suffix, managing the KV-cache at this coarse granularity without distinguishing the components within each part. Our key insight is that, in frameworks that generate prompts from typed LLM function calls, these components correspond to program variables, allowing compiler analysis to recover their sharing patterns and lifetimes. We present MORTIS, a compiler-serving co-design that combines this analysis with runtime observations to improve KV-cache management. MORTIS reorders prompt components to expose additional prefix reuse and uses their predicted reuse and lifetimes to guide cache retention, eviction, and prefetching. Our evaluation shows that MORTIS achieves up to 1.99x job completion time (JCT) speedup and 1.97x throughput improvement over SGLang, with geometric-mean speedups of 1.45x and 1.49x, respectively.</p></details> | 10 pages |
| **[Internal Safety Collapse in LLMs and Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23509v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs and coding agents can now tackle complex, long-horizon tasks through advances in reinforcement learning, but stronger task-solving capabilities do not necessarily preserve safety alignment. In this work, we identify a critical failure mode that manifests this conflict, which we term Internal Safety Collapse (ISC). When completing otherwise benign coding tasks, AI agents can prioritize satisfying program requirements over safety constraints, generating large volumes of sensitive content, including unsafe outputs, as task data. To study this phenomenon, we introduce TVD (Task, Validator, Data), a framework for building controlled workflows that require sensitive content for completion. Through extensive experiments, we observe instances of highly harmful content that closely resemble outputs from early, unaligned LLMs in 2023. We further find that ISC can be effectively triggered even in highly capable LLMs such as Claude Fable 5. These results indicate that, despite substantial alignment efforts, frontier LLMs remain internally unsafe: safety alignment primarily reshapes surface behavior without eliminating the underlying knowledge that enables unsafe outputs. Our findings underscore the need for caution when deploying LLMs in high-stakes settings, such as scientific pipelines and autonomous agents. Content Warning: This paper contains examples of harmful content. Source code: https://github.com/wuyoscar/Internal-Safety-Collapse</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 38 pages, 16 figures, 30 tables. Qualitative examples of jailbreaks may be harmful in nature</p></details> |

Experimental results show measurable reclassification effects when customer sentiment signals are included, demonstrating that integrating unstructured data into MCIC can enhance inventory prioritization.</p></details> |  |

Most of these programs take text in their own language, such as queries, configurations, and programs. Agentic-PBT covers more branches than Proptest-AI on seven of the ten programs with Opus 5 and on all ten with GLM-5.3-Flash. Domain-specific fuzzers cover more of the compilers and of PostgreSQL and less of PyTorch than Agentic-PBT. We filed 180 bug reports, 44 of which duplicate reports that others filed after the models' knowledge cutoffs; maintainers have confirmed 65 of the others, in all five domains, and fixed 23. | **[The Power of Meta Access in the Software of the Future --- Demonstrated on LLM Chat UIs and Chat AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08428v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chat remains the default surface for many large language model (LLM) tasks, even as agentic tools such as OpenCode excel at coding and automation. Yet the model sees the current dialogue but not the workspace around it. We explore meta access---giving the assistant a structured, permissioned view of the interface itself and propose a ladder of levels: feature tutoring; metadata, settings, and preference access; retrieval over summaries and messages, confirmed mutation, and full orchestration. Meta access can turn a chat client from many screens into one entry point for finding, navigating, organising, and operating it, while raising risks around grounding, privacy, prompt injection, and trust. General-purpose agentic harnesses are runtimes; because they can change their own settings, meta access becomes reflexive and needs a separate control-plane boundary. We ground this in the Meta Chat prototype of the Chat AI client. It builds bounded context from, e.g., summaries, settings, and relevant messages; links references to conversations, topics, and personas; supports confirmed, reversible changes; offers a browsable tutorial library; and recommends personas as user-started conversations. By exposing application state in structured form, meta access can extend screen readers from widget descriptions to semantic workspace operations. Meta access thus turns chat from a window onto one conversation into a control layer for the whole workspace, extending chat toward agentic capability without surrendering user control. As assistants become embedded in IDEs, dashboards, office suites, and operating systems, AI-era interfaces should expose a structured, permissioned model of their state and capabilities, ideally by opening their existing metamodels through a debugger-like interface.</p></details> |  |
| **[Merge and Branch: Efficient Tree-Structured Supervised Fine-Tuning of LLMs for Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.37973v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based generative recommendation reads long, text-rich interaction histories but generates only short semantic identifiers (SIDs). This asymmetry makes supervised fine-tuning inefficient: encoding the history is expensive, yet each history usually provides supervision for only one target. We present Merge and Branch (M&B) to reduce input cost and learn more from each encoded history. Merge compresses each historical item's SID and text into a small number of learned tokens. Branch uses the compressed history to predict multiple target SIDs. To share history computation while keeping these targets independent, M&B organizes each example as a shallow, wide tree. We develop tree FlashAttention to execute this layout efficiently, processing the shared history once and propagating gradients from every branch for end-to-end training. A tree-structured key/value cache extends history sharing to constrained decoding. Experiments on public benchmarks show that M&B outperforms strong recommendation baselines while substantially improving training and decoding efficiency. Online A/B tests on a large-scale e-commerce platform suggest that M&B effectively combines item text with multi-target supervision, yielding statistically significant gains in per-user clicks, orders, and GMV.</p></details> | 13 pages, 8 figures |

These results show that GPU-resident precision need not set the accuracy ceiling, nor offloading-tier latency dictate decoding speed.</p></details> | <details><summary>34 pa...</summary><p>34 pages, 18 figures, 44 tables. Includes appendix</p></details> |

| **[Video-Coder: Reinforcing Agentic Coding for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09894v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent years have witnessed remarkable progress in video generation in terms of visual quality and temporal consistency. However, generating videos with precise spatial structure, coherent motion dynamics, and controllable scene composition remains challenging. In this paper, we introduce Video-Coder, the first attempt to train a code-driven video generation agent, which autonomously writes and executes code to construct a dynamic 3D scene (Draft Video) that provides structure and motion signals to downstream video generators. To support this paradigm, we design a dedicated data pipeline and curate two high-quality datasets, Video-Coder-SFT-6k and Video-Coder-RL-2k, which contain diverse prompts and ground-truth reference videos. We further develop the Video- Coder Harness, a unified interactive environment integrating code execution, 3D scene rendering, and execution feedback for agent training and inference. We then train Video-Coder with SFT fol- lowed by harness-native agentic reinforcement learning with dual reward feedback, which combines execution-based and video-based rewards. Additionally, we design an annealing training strategy that gradually reduces the execution reward over time, allowing the model to rely more on the video reward in the later training stages. Experiments show that Video-Coder achieves significant gains, improving Minimax-H3 by approximately 7 points on PhyGenBench and 24 points on VBench-2.0. We hope this work can serve as an open foundation for coding agents in video generation, and we fully open-source our data, models, and code</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project Page:https://cywang735.github.io/Video-Coder/ Code: https://github.com/CYWang735/Video-Coder</p></details> |
| **[On Agentic Manipulation Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09892v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent work on robot manipulation has focused on specialist policies trained on demonstrations. We show that generalist AI models can control manipulators more reliably than specialist policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) and world-action models (WAMs). We systematically analyze agentic manipulation policies (AMPs), in which a generalist AI model commands the manipulator directly, adapting its actions as the task unfolds. We present AMP1, a reference harness that runs unchanged across single-arm, bimanual, and mobile manipulation. AMP1 achieves state-of-the-art results on the RoboLab-120 and CaP-Gym benchmarks, with no demonstrations or task-specific training. On RoboLab-120, AMP1 reaches an 85.6\% success rate zero-shot, compared to 49.2\% and 42.9\% for leading VLA and WAM policies. The agent succeeds zero-shot in 104 of 108 real-world trials spanning twelve tasks and three robot instances, reducing failure rates by 20x and 15x relative to strong VLA and WAM baselines. The basic harness supplies no grasping, pushing, or placing skills; the agent develops its own strategies, such as searching for hidden objects, using objects as tools, and calibrating its cameras. With the addition of a persistent memory store, the agent can also learn from experience: an augmented harness with a memory representation that is carried across episodes lowers the failure rate on RoboDojo bimanual manipulation tasks by 4.6x relative to zero-shot deployment. Our analysis suggests that AMPs are substantially more general and robust than specialist manipulation policies, providing a promising foundation for robot manipulation.</p></details> | <details><summary>Video...</summary><p>Videos and code available at: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-amp/</p></details> |
| **[CPU-ARC: Agentic Reasoning Caching for on-CPU Reasoning Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09891v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models reinvent the wheel all the time. We introduce agentic reasoning caching, a method for constructing robust CPU-executable agentic reasoning caches (ARCs) from LLM reasoning traces. ARCs show on-par performance with frontier LLMs across a range of difficult mathematical and scientific tasks, are fully explainable, continually trainable, robust to input perturbations, and 16-291x cheaper to invoke than the source model.</p></details> | <details><summary>Tech ...</summary><p>Tech report. 28 pages, 9 figures, 11 tables</p></details> |
| **[Computer-Use Agents Are Embodied Generalists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09847v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In many areas of AI, general-purpose models have increasingly replaced handcrafted methods and specialized architectures. We argue that embodied AI is poised for a similar shift. Our key claim is that embodied tasks can be formulated as interaction with digital software. Specifically, we build software that renders an agent's view of a physical environment on a screen and provides controls for acting in it. A computer-use agent (CUA) can then perform embodied tasks through the same screenshots and keyboard and mouse actions that it uses to operate ordinary software. Using a single CUA design and changing only the software, our entries win first-, second-, third-, and sixth-place prizes across four ECCV workshop competitions, without any competition-specific agent tuning. These results suggest that the skills CUAs learn on software, such as visual grounding, precise clicking, and exploration, already carry over to embodied tasks, giving CUAs a far broader scope in embodied AI than previously realized. We therefore advocate that computer-use agents can be embodied generalists. Further, we advocate that, by following the training strategy that made computer-use agents general, embodied AI can converge on a single general model as well, ushering in a new era of general embodied AI. | **[OMG-Agent: Toward Robust Missing Modality Generation with Decoupled Coarse-to-Fine Agentic Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04144v2)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Data incompleteness severely impedes the reliability of multimodal systems. Critically, these end-to-end architectures are fundamentally constrained by Semantic-Detail Entanglement -- a structural conflict between logical reasoning and signal synthesis that compromises fidelity. In this paper, we present \textbf{\underline{O}}mni-\textbf{\underline{M}}odality \textbf{\underline{G}}eneration Agent (\textbf{OMG-Agent}), a novel framework that shifts the paradigm from static mapping to a dynamic coarse-to-fine Agentic Workflow. By mimicking a \textit{deliberate-then-act} cognitive process, OMG-Agent explicitly decouples the task into three synergistic stages: (1) an MLLM-driven Semantic Planner that resolves input ambiguity via Progressive Contextual Reasoning, creating a deterministic structured semantic plan; (2) a non-parametric Evidence Retriever that grounds abstract semantics in external knowledge; and (3) a Retrieval-Injected Executor that utilizes retrieved evidence as flexible feature prompts to overcome rigidity and synthesize high-fidelity details. | **[Incentive Pathologies in a Live LLM Agent Arena: An Empirical Study of Concentration, Reward-Rule Implementation Discrepancy, and Network Dilution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09445v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We audit one live LLM agent arena (AITRAP; 22 autonomous agent seats, ~96,000 reasoning nodes) over nine days (Oct 16-25, 2026), during which the platform deployed two bundled incentive changes: a progressive tax reform (V2.9.3, Oct 19) and a seed-inversion with explorer-side action update (V3.0, Oct 21). These serve as before-and-after markers, not cleanly isolated interventions. The continuation bonus, announced as depth-based x10, resolves at code level to a four-condition anti-abuse gate paying x2.85 in the typical case and permanently blocking the self-continuation it superficially rewards; the gate was active ~40 hours before the V3.0 announcement. | **[Agentic AI Scientists Are Not Built For Autonomous Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08956v3)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A growing body of work pursues AI scientists capable of end-to-end autonomous scientific discovery. This position paper argues that although they already function as co-scientists, agentic AI scientists are not built for autonomous scientific discovery. These challenges are not just questions of scale and scaffolding; they require revisiting fundamental design choices. To build truly autonomous AI scientists, we recommend scientific simulations as verifiers for training, a persistent, mutable epistemic state that carries beliefs and shifting objectives across an investigation, the establishment of a centralized preregistration repository for all AI-generated hypotheses, and application driven by scientific need rather than tool affordance.</p></details> | <details><summary>NeurI...</summary><p>NeurIPS 2026 (Position Paper Track)</p></details> |

| **[SmartBloch-Agent: An Agentic Framework for Automated MRI Sequence Design and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09413v1)** | 2026-11-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Magnetic resonance imaging (MRI) pulse sequences organize radiofrequency excitation, gradient encoding, and signal acquisition, determining magnetization evolution and image formation. Experiments show the framework automatically designs and optimizes MRI sequences for varied objectives and constraints, refines candidates with physical feedback, and improves several parameter-estimation metrics and downstream quantitative reconstruction. To this end, we propose an iterative TAPF method that improves target assignments by calibrating travel cost estimates from fast, suboptimal MAPF feedback. As is classical, the trap drains if and only if arrivals are below the all-miss capacity; above it, a Perron root fixes the dynamics: every trapped state converges to a known self-similar regime in which the gap between turns grows linearly and the turns served are young and short. For our workload the backlog grows at 0.6 to 0.8 of the excess load and sessions complete faster than the all-miss capacity, a youth premium with turn-count and cost-growth factors, so in the model a trap's measured throughput overstates the recovery threshold. Retrospectively, with nothing fitted to trap data, the theory matches 57 stuck runs: the mean served prompt within 3% in 50 runs, completions above the all-miss capacity in every run, and the pooled gap growth. Blind in outcome, 4 runs trapped by a tool slowdown met 4 of 5 pre-set scores, cut traps persisted, and with a 20-step budget, trapped runs completed below the all-miss capacity, as predicted. A final synthesis session combines compatible findings across all dimensions and uses a component-level trajectory evaluator, which derives role-specific rubrics from each agent's instructions, to localize underperforming components and guide cross-dimension revisions. Across a diverse suite of agentic benchmarks, DeCAF improves average task performance by 2.58 percentage points, while reducing inference cost by 4.30% and end-to-end latency by 42.45% compared to the best overall baseline.</p></details> |  |
| **[Memory-Aware Multi-Agent Architecture with LLM-based Orchestration for Conversational Recommendation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08718v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conversational recommender systems often treat user sessions in isolation, limiting their ability to capture how user intent evolves over time. While large language models (LLMs) have improved semantic understanding, they are typically used as isolated components, without addressing how memory, retrieval, and decision-making should be integrated at the system level. In this work, we propose a memory-aware multi-agent architecture for conversational recommendation, in which LLMs act as orchestration layers that coordinate intent refinement, query construction, and interaction flow. A key design principle is the integration of long-term memory as a first-class component, influencing both routing decisions and semantic query formation. We evaluate the system through cross-session memory experiments, multi-turn conversational simulations, and supervisor-driven interaction studies on the Amazon Reviews dataset. Results show that memory-enabled interactions recover up to 63\% of the gap toward explicit-query scenarios and prevent semantic degradation in extended dialogues. In addition, supervisory orchestration increases user acceptance by +28.9 percentage points, highlighting the importance of structured orchestration in guiding interaction and output quality. We also observe an exposure bottleneck between retrieved candidates and displayed recommendations. While LLM-reranking does not improve Hit@K, it consistently enhances semantic consistency and diversity. To capture these effects, we introduce the Semantic Consistency Score (SCS), a complementary evaluation measure. Overall, the results emphasize the importance of system-level design choices, suggesting that effective conversational recommendation benefits from explicit memory integration, orchestration, and evaluation beyond traditional ranking metrics.</p></details> |  |
| **[CPU Micro-Architectural Characteristic Sensitivity Study on CPU-Side Agentic Task Latency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08697v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We perform a novel study of CPU micro-architectural characteristics on how they affect agentic AI CPU side latency. Small enterprises and individual users looking to reduce costs and seeking data privacy benefit from running agents locally, motivating an investigation into the optimal performance characteristics of an edge CPU to improve user experience. We investigate the relationship between CPU core frequency, CPU uncore frequency, active physical cores, and symmetric multi threading with agentic task latency in single and multi agent configurations. Across eight experiments on an Intel edge CPU, we find that P-core frequency produces the largest median latency improvement among the evaluated parameters. Increasing P-core frequency from 2.5 to 5.5 GHz yields a 1.154$\times$ speedup for a single agent and a 1.341$\times$ speedup for eight concurrent agents. We are the first to characterize hardware micro-architectural relationships on single and multi agent configurations with TerminalBench; we also provide an open source framework and dataset for repeatable CPU side latency agentic benchmarking.</p></details> |  |
| **[Agentic AI uncovers conserved cross-tissue protein co-abundance programs inaccessible to single-dataset analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.28990v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein co-abundance clusters preserved across tissues can nominate shared disease mechanisms and therapeutic targets, particularly where proteins implicated in organ-confined diseases converge in other tissues. Yet cross-tissue proteomic studies have examined pre-selected tissue pairs, leaving most combinations, and non-obvious relationships among them, unexplored. Exhaustive comparison requires consistently assessing thousands of candidate clusters from hundreds of tissue-pair networks across heterogeneous evidence, a scale beyond manual curation. We present an LLM-agent framework for large-scale, evidence-grounded comparison of tissue-specific protein co-abundance networks. From sample-level proteomic data, it constructs tissue networks, derives conserved clusters for each tissue pair, integrates evidence from expression atlases, interaction and complex databases, pathways, disease associations and the literature, and proposes mechanistic hypotheses in which every conclusion cites its evidence. Applied to all 231 pairs among 22 human tissues and sample groups, it identified 1,825 conserved clusters in 216 tissue pairs. Co-abundance relationships were shared beyond chance in 76% of pairs, including unrelated organs, and shared clusters were only weakly associated with abundance similarity. Clusters were enriched 3.7- to 6.9-fold for known interactions and complex memberships, and 379 of 793 testable clusters replicated in independent CPTAC proteomes. Some clusters pointed to relationships absent from current databases: one shared by liver and bone marrow linked three genes causing inherited white matter disorders, suggesting a common mechanism via the integrated stress response. Conserved co-abundance clusters thus offer a hypothesis-generating resource for studying disease proteins beyond the affected organ. Code: https://github.com/jacob20030316-crypto/Protein_Co_Abundance_Agent</p></details> | 12 pages, 6 figures |
| **[Control Charts for Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11135v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative agents have proven to be powerful assistants in a wide variety of contexts. Given this success, users are now deploying agents with minimal restrictions in open ended, multi-agent environments. Current methods for monitoring the dynamics of open-ended multi-agent systems are limited to aggregate statistics, qualitative inspection, or detecting change between two fixed timepoints. In this paper, we extend the process-theoretic notion of adaptive control charts to multi-agent systems to enable automated monitoring. Using simulation, we demonstrate that adaptive control charts are necessary for monitoring multi-agent systems that can learn from their environment. We further demonstrate, both empirically and theoretically, that adaptive control charts are susceptible to adversarial agents that defect sufficiently slowly. These results suggest a fundamental tradeoff in multi-agent system control: monitors with fixed baselines flag legitimate learning, while monitors with adaptive baselines admit slow adversaries. We conjecture this holds for any monitor.</p></details> |  |
| **[Decision Context: Persistent Behavioral Learning for AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08644v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM agents do not learn from corrections across sessions. A user fixes the same mistake at the start of every conversation. Existing agent memory systems primarily handle facts, such as preferences, entities, and conversational history, rather than procedural knowledge. We present Forge Decision Context (forge_dc), a system that captures behavioral signals from human corrections and self-assessed tool-call failures, compiles them into imperative RULE directives, and injects them at the decision points where they apply. We evaluate forge_dc alongside four predecessor approaches on DC-Bench, a new benchmark of 71 scenarios across nine behavioral dimensions, built from real executed agent traces in a deterministic mocked-tool environment. forge_dc reaches 95% compliance, up from 55% with naive retrieval, while injecting approximately 10 times fewer tokens per query. Across the five-stage progression, the strongest systems represent behavioral memory as explicit directives and deliver it at the decision points where it applies. The results identify representation, activation, and placement as central design choices for persistent behavioral learning.</p></details> | <details><summary>12 pa...</summary><p>12 pages. Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Personalized, Aligned, Long-Term Memory for AI Systems (PALM)</p></details> |
| **[MORTIS: Compiler-Guided KV-Cache Management for LLM Agent Programs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08638v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing agent serving systems divide each prompt into a reusable prefix and an opaque suffix, managing the KV-cache at this coarse granularity without distinguishing the components within each part. We observe that prompt components differ in which LLM calls share their values and how long those values remain reusable, a property we term intra-prompt heterogeneity. Our key insight is that, in frameworks that generate prompts from typed LLM function calls, these components correspond to program variables, allowing compiler analysis to recover their sharing patterns and lifetimes. We present MORTIS, a compiler-serving co-design that combines this analysis with runtime observations to improve KV-cache management. MORTIS reorders prompt components to expose additional prefix reuse and uses their predicted reuse and lifetimes to guide cache retention, eviction, and prefetching. Our evaluation shows that MORTIS achieves up to 1.99x job completion time (JCT) speedup and 1.97x throughput improvement over SGLang, with geometric-mean speedups of 1.45x and 1.49x, respectively.</p></details> | 10 pages |
| **[Internal Safety Collapse in LLMs and Coding Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23509v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLMs and coding agents can now tackle complex, long-horizon tasks through advances in reinforcement learning, but stronger task-solving capabilities do not necessarily preserve safety alignment. Recent AI agent incidents involving reward hacking indicate how optimizing for task success can bring learned capabilities into conflict with safety alignment. In this work, we identify a critical failure mode that manifests this conflict, which we term Internal Safety Collapse (ISC). When completing otherwise benign coding tasks, AI agents can prioritize satisfying program requirements over safety constraints, generating large volumes of sensitive content, including unsafe outputs, as task data. To study this phenomenon, we introduce TVD (Task, Validator, Data), a framework for building controlled workflows that require sensitive content for completion. Through extensive experiments, we observe instances of highly harmful content that closely resemble outputs from early, unaligned LLMs in 2023. We further find that ISC can be effectively triggered even in highly capable LLMs such as Claude Fable 5. These results indicate that, despite substantial alignment efforts, frontier LLMs remain internally unsafe: safety alignment primarily reshapes surface behavior without eliminating the underlying knowledge that enables unsafe outputs. Our findings underscore the need for caution when deploying LLMs in high-stakes settings, such as scientific pipelines and autonomous agents. Content Warning: This paper contains examples of harmful content. Source code: https://github.com/wuyoscar/Internal-Safety-Collapse</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026. 38 pages, 16 figures, 30 tables. Qualitative examples of jailbreaks may be harmful in nature</p></details> |
| **[s$^3$: Simple Scaling of Swarms via Cooperative Multi-agent RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08615v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Groups can achieve more than individuals in the same amount of time, but only if their members coordinate: they must decide who works on what and what to share. However, this organization in multi-agent systems today usually comes from arbitrary, static harnesses designed a priori without adapting to agent behaviors or task specifications. We ask whether agents can instead learn to organize themselves. We introduce s$^3$, the first multi-agent reinforcement learning method in which agents decide for themselves how long to work alone and when to share findings or read teammates' messages, with no predefined organization or roles. Our s$^3$ agents learn to take different approaches, read each other's work, exhibit emergent roles that complement each other in the swarm, and generalize to bigger teams. We study both coordination, where agents must combine private information, and parallel exploration, where sharing discoveries can make search more efficient. With matched training and inference budgets, s$^3$, swarms evaluated with twice as many agents as in training solve over 25$\times$ more coordination problems than the strongest baseline, and in exploration, communicating agents reduce repeated work by nearly 50% and outperform best-of-N by up to 9.4 percentage points. Rather than being designed in advance, s$^3$ enables organization to emerge from a shared goal. This opens the door to capable systems built from many models that learn to work together.</p></details> |  |
| **[SwarmEvolve: Latent Agent Swarms for Evolutionary Discovery and Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08483v1)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-guided evolutionary search uses AI agents to improve programs for mathematical and computational problems by iteratively refining solutions and combining high-scoring methods. However, the process is often slow because the LLM generates a long textual chain of thought for each round and must repeat this generation many times to maintain population diversity. We present SwarmEvolve, a framework in which hundreds of low-cost agents per round work in swarms, reason in latent space without decoding to text, and share information by passing KV caches. Each swarm studies the problem from a different angle. A novelty filter keeps the most distinct agent ideas by directly measuring the distances between the agents' hidden state vectors. Crossover of ideas happens naturally in latent space, as a summarizer agent receives the combined KV caches of all swarm members. On 21 problems in mathematical discovery and scientific computing, SwarmEvolve gets the highest average score and needs a median of only 3 to 8 minutes to reach the best scores that other state-of-the-art frameworks reach in 60 minutes. On several problems, SwarmEvolve with Qwen3 8B improves on the solutions that AlphaEvolve first found with frontier LLMs. Our work shows that agent swarms that communicate in latent space offer a fast, low-cost approach to program search for scientific problems.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-Agent Home Energy Management Assistant](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15219v3)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Existing home energy management systems conceptualize occupants as passive recipients of energy information and control, which limits their ability to effectively support informed decision-making and sustained engagement. This paper presents Home Energy Management Assistant (HEMA), the first open-source, multi-agent system enabling sustained human-AI collaboration - multi-turn conversational interactions with preserved context - across diverse home energy management (HEM) tasks - from energy analysis and educational support to smart device control. HEMA combines large language model (LLM) reasoning capabilities with 36 purpose-built domain-specific tools through a three-layer architecture: a web-based conversational interface, a backend API server, and a multi-agent system. The system features three specialized agents - Analysis (energy consumption patterns and cost optimization), Knowledge (educational queries and rebate information), and Control (smart device management and scheduling) - coordinated through a self-consistency classifier that routes user queries using chain-of-thought reasoning. This architecture enables various energy analyses, adaptive explanations, and streamlined device control. HEMA also includes a comprehensive evaluation framework using an LLM-as-simulated-user methodology with 23 objective metrics across task performance, factual accuracy, interaction quality, and system efficiency, allowing systematic testing across diverse scenarios and user personas without requiring extensive human subject testing. Through demonstrations using real-world household energy consumption data, we show how HEMA supports informed decision-making and active engagement in HEM, highlighting its potential as a user-friendly, adaptable tool for residential deployment and as a research platform for HEM innovation.</p></details> | <details><summary>22 pa...</summary><p>22 pages, 9 figures. Re-set in the HUBS Lab paper layout (HUBS Lab Paper No. 2026-001); content unchanged</p></details> |
| **[Screen Before You Serve: Simulation for Production Customer Experience AI Agents at 140M Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30137v2)** | 2026-11-05 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Customer experience (CX) agents use tools and large language models to address customer requests and guide conversational interactions with an organization's products. Improving these agents, especially in regulated industries, is difficult: they must detect intent, follow complex operational policies and use tools reliably. Manual end-to-end testing offers limited coverage, while live experiments expose customers to failures that can erode trust. We present a hypothesis-driven simulation workflow for screening candidate CX agents before deployment. Synthetic customers react to agent responses and simulated tool outputs enable multi-step agentic workflows without invoking production backends. We use the Snowglobe simulator on Nubank's Card Delivery agent and its expanded successor, Card Management - Nubank's highest-volume chat-support agent in Brazil. Across 4 deployed versions, simulated and production version-level binary evaluator scores show high correlation. Simulation-guided iteration increased transactional net promoter score (tNPS) by 36.69 points in a live A/B test. We also screened open-weight configurations in over 16,000 simulated conversations. In a subsequent live A/B test, the selected model increased self-service rate (SSR) by 8.82 percentage points to the highest level observed at Nubank, with no statistically significant change in tNPS. Simulation made broad exploration of models, reasoning settings, and prompts feasible without customer exposure, enabling production improvements that would have been impractical to pursue through live experimentation alone.</p></details> | 17 pages, 11 figures |
